Nothing comes to mind clearly enough for me to say yes.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function day to day?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. It’s not just that I’m moving slowly or having a bad few days — things are actually piling up in a way that’s starting to scare me.\n\nI’ve got bills I haven’t dealt with, not because I don’t know they’re there, but because I open the envelope or see the reminder and then I just… can’t make myself follow through. Same with things around the house. Little projects or repairs that I used to be able to chip away at are just sitting there, and every time I look at them I feel worse, but that still doesn’t make me do them.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve basically stopped showing up. I used to have book club, and sometimes rides with a couple people — not a huge circle, but there were a few people I counted on seeing. I’ve let that fall away. I either don’t respond or I tell myself I’ll go and then I don’t.\n\nI guess the part that makes me feel like I can’t just brush this off is… I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. That felt different. Like it crossed some line from “I’m behind on things” into “something is really not okay.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts of not wanting to wake up feel tied specifically to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s only that. I mean, my daughter leaving for college is part of the background, I’m sure — it’s a big change, and I don’t want to pretend it doesn’t affect me. But the thoughts don’t feel as simple as, “She left, so now I don’t want to wake up.”\n\nIt’s more like… I’ve been having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not necessarily in some dramatic way, but like going to sleep and not having to face everything again. And that scares me to say out loud, because it doesn’t feel like the way I usually think.\n\nThe thing that really frightened me was when I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was trying to make them understandable for someone else. Like I was getting things ready. I don’t even know if I fully realized what I was doing at first, but then it hit me, and I thought, why am I organizing this like somebody else is going to need to take over?\n\nSo… I can’t say it’s specifically just about my daughter leaving. It feels bigger and darker than that, and that’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more unsettling parts, because I used to be very good at that kind of thing. I mean, I spent years managing engineering spreadsheets — complicated ones, with formulas and dependencies and all these little details — and I didn’t really have to think about thinking, if that makes sense. I could just sit down and work through it.\n\nNow I’ll be looking at something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just… freeze. I can stare at it for half an hour and still not decide what the next step is. It’s not that I don’t understand what a bill is, obviously, or that I don’t know we need groceries. It’s more like my brain won’t organize the information into an action. I’ll read the same line over and over, or compare two things that shouldn’t be hard to compare, and somehow I still can’t make a decision.\n\nIt makes me feel embarrassed, honestly. Like I’ve lost access to a part of myself that used to be reliable.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had hard periods before, like anyone — stretches where I was stressed or down or not myself — but whether it was depressed nearly every day for a full two weeks before this past month… I don’t know.\n\nNothing is coming to mind that I can point to and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing, for that length of time. I’m trying not to rewrite my past just because things feel so bad now. There were definitely times I was unhappy or under strain, but I don’t know that I would describe it that way. This feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say I’ve been here before.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, do you remember ever having a previous episode like this?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve had bad times before, like I said — stressful periods, times where I was unhappy or overwhelmed — but I don’t remember ever having something that felt like this.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say “never” if there was some period I’m forgetting or minimizing, but nothing stands out to me as a previous episode like this. This past month feels different in a way that’s hard to compare to anything earlier.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not good. Really not good.\n\nI can get into bed, but then I’ll just lie there for hours. The house gets so quiet at night that it almost makes everything louder in my head, if that makes sense. I’m lying there listening to every little creak or the hum of something in the walls, and I’m trying to make myself sleep, but that just seems to make it worse.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up around three or four in the morning. It’s almost always that same window. I’ll come awake and for a few seconds I’ll think maybe I can just turn over and go back to sleep, but then my mind starts going. I mean, I’ve had hard periods before, like anyone — stretches where I was stressed or down or not myself — but whether it was depressed nearly every day for a full two weeks before this past month… I don’t know.\n\nNothing is coming to mind that I can point to and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing, for that length of time. I’m trying not to rewrite my past just because things feel so bad now. There were definitely times I was unhappy or under strain, but I don’t know that I would describe it that way. This feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say I’ve been here before.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, do you remember ever having a previous episode like this?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve had bad times before, like I said — stressful periods, times where I was unhappy or overwhelmed — but I don’t remember ever having something that felt like this.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say “never” if there was some period I’m forgetting or minimizing, but nothing stands out to me as a previous episode like this. This past month feels different in a way that’s hard to compare to anything earlier.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not good. Really not good.\n\nI can get into bed, but then I’ll just lie there for hours. The house gets so quiet at night that it almost makes everything louder in my head, if that makes sense. I’m lying there listening to every little creak or the hum of something in the walls, and I’m trying to make myself sleep, but that just seems to make it worse.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up around three or four in the morning. It’s almost always that same window. I’ll come awake and for a few seconds I’ll think maybe I can just turn over and go back to sleep, but then my mind starts going. It runs through everything I’ve messed up, everything I should have done differently, things I said or didn’t say, decisions I made. Once that starts, that’s it. I just lie there staring into the dark, going over it again and again.\n\nSo I’m in bed for a long time, but it doesn’t feel like real rest. It feels like I spend half the night waiting to fall asleep and the other half trying not to think.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from your daughter leaving for college?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can't shake this feeling that I failed as a father and as a useful person, and it keeps looping in my head until I feel like I don't deserve much of a future.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I think so. I mean, her leaving for college is part of it, obviously — I don’t want to pretend it isn’t. It hit me harder than I expected, and I miss having her around in the house.

But this feels… bigger than that. Or darker than that. It’s not just “my daughter moved out and I’m sad.” It turns into this whole judgment on my life, like I failed as a father somehow, even though I know sending your child to college is supposed to be a good thing. I keep going over things I did wrong, things I didn’t do enough of, ways I should have been better. And then it spreads from that into this feeling that I’m not really useful to anyone anymore.

That’s honestly part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this thought that I’ve failed — as a father, as a person, just as someone who’s supposed to have some purpose. It loops in my head, and after a while it stops feeling like a thought and starts feeling like the truth. Like I don’t really deserve much of a future, if that makes sense.

So yes… I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just her leaving. It feels like that may have opened the door, but what came through feels much heavier than that.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.9, F43.2, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is this feeling I can’t get away from, that I’ve failed. Failed as a father, especially, and just failed at being a useful person in general. It’s not like a passing thought, either. It just keeps going around in my head, over and over, like I’m constantly reviewing all the ways I’ve let people down.\n\nAnd I know people might say, “Well, everyone makes mistakes,” but it doesn’t feel like that. It feels deeper than that, like it says something about who I am. I keep thinking I should have done more, been better, been someone my family could be proud of, or at least someone who had a place in things. Instead I feel like I’ve become… I don’t know, kind of pointless.\n\nThat’s why I came in. Because when it gets looping like that, I start feeling like I don’t really deserve much of a future. Like maybe I’ve used up whatever chances I had to be worthwhile.\n\nDoctor: When you say you don’t deserve much of a future, have you been thinking about ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not in the way I always imagined people meant that, like some clear decision or a plan laid out in front of me. But yes, I’ve been having thoughts like… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep showing up to my own life, that would be a relief.\n\nAnd it scared me, honestly, because the other day I caught myself going over my finances, not in the normal way, not like paying bills or budgeting, but more like… making sure things would be understandable for someone else if I wasn’t here. And when I realized what I was doing, it kind of hit me. Like, why am I thinking about it like that? Why am I organizing things as if I’m getting them ready for someone else?\n\nSo… yes. Those thoughts have been there. I don’t like saying it out loud, but they have.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think it really started about a month ago, when my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI knew it was coming, obviously. It wasn’t some surprise—there were applications, visits, packing, all of that. And I told myself I was happy for her, because I am, I really am. But after she left, the house felt different in a way I didn’t expect. It was like this whole part of my life just… closed. And once she was gone, all I could think about was whether I’d done enough as her father, whether I’d wasted the time I had, whether she was leaving partly because she needed distance from me.\n\nSo, yeah, if I’m honest, that’s when this became constant. Around a month ago. Since she left.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much so. I used to have things I genuinely looked forward to, little things, but they mattered. I’ve had this model railway I’ve been working on for years—tracks, little buildings, wiring things up, painting tiny details. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but it used to calm me down. I could lose an afternoon in it and feel like I’d actually made something.\n\nNow it’s just sitting there on the table half-built. Pieces out, tools still there, like I meant to come back to it, but I don’t. I look at it and it just feels… pointless. Like I can’t understand why I cared about it before.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least text during the games if we weren’t in the same room. That was kind of our thing. Now even opening a game on TV feels like too much, or like there’s no reason to. I’ll think, “Maybe I should put it on,” and then I just don’t, because it feels empty before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the day nearly every day” sounds so specific, and I haven’t exactly been keeping track by the hour.\n\nI know I’ve been feeling low a lot. It’s not like one bad afternoon where I can say, “Oh, that was a rough day,” and then I bounce back. It’s more like this weight is there when I wake up, and then sometimes it fades into the background if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really leave. I’ll be sitting there doing nothing in particular and realize I’ve just been feeling awful for… I don’t even know how long.\n\nSo, yes, I think so. Or close to that. It feels like it’s been there most days, and for a lot of each day. I don’t want to exaggerate, but I also don’t want to pretend it’s just occasional, because it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like… I haven’t had someone say, “You’re talking slower,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I do feel like I take longer to answer sometimes because I’m trying to get my thoughts together, and I probably pause more than I used to. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as moving or speaking noticeably slowly. I haven’t really been watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I can’t say for sure. It’s not something I’ve clearly noticed the way I’ve noticed some of the other things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has decreased?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think it has. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to, or maybe I just don’t notice it until it’s already late. Most days I don’t bother making a proper meal. I’ll sort of tell myself I’ll do it in a bit, and then the day just goes by, and by the time I realize I haven’t eaten, I don’t have the energy to make anything real.\n\nSo I end up with toast, or a bowl of cereal, something like that. Just whatever’s easiest and doesn’t feel like… an undertaking. And I’ve had to tighten my belt lately, which made me stop and think, because I hadn’t really been paying attention to it as “losing weight” exactly. It’s more like I’m just not eating normally.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had times in my life when I was unhappy, or under a lot of stress, or just… not myself for a while. I think most people probably have stretches like that, and when I look back it’s hard to know what counts as “depressed like this” versus just going through something difficult.\n\nWeeks at a time… maybe? But I don’t remember anything that feels clearly the same as what this has been like recently. I don’t want to say no too quickly, because my memory for those periods isn’t perfect, and I may have minimized things at the time. But I also can’t point to a definite past episode and say, “Yes, that was just like this.”\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Nothing stands out to me clearly before this past month in the same way.\n\nDoctor: At any earlier time in your life, did you ever have a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking it a little more specifically now — like not just “a rough patch,” but two weeks or more where it was nearly every day, either feeling depressed or not really being interested in anything.\n\nWhen I think back, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing well, where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if it was nearly every day for two weeks, or if I’m sort of filling in the blanks afterward. It’s hard for me to separate what was actual depression from just being unhappy or stressed or withdrawn for a while.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a period before this where I’d say, yes, that definitely happened for at least two weeks nearly every day. But I also don’t feel completely confident saying no, because I may not have recognized it that way at the time. So… I guess my best answer is that I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind clearly enough for me to say yes.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function day to day?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. It’s not just that I’m moving slowly or having a bad few days — things are actually piling up in a way that’s starting to scare me.\n\nI’ve got bills I haven’t dealt with, not because I don’t know they’re there, but because I open the envelope or see the reminder and then I just… can’t make myself follow through. Same with things around the house. Little projects or repairs that I used to be able to chip away at are just sitting there, and every time I look at them I feel worse, but that still doesn’t make me do them.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve basically stopped showing up. I used to have book club, and sometimes rides with a couple people — not a huge circle, but there were a few people I counted on seeing. I’ve let that fall away. I either don’t respond or I tell myself I’ll go and then I don’t.\n\nI guess the part that makes me feel like I can’t just brush this off is… I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. That felt different. Like it crossed some line from “I’m behind on things” into “something is really not okay.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts of not wanting to wake up feel tied specifically to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s only that. I mean, my daughter leaving for college is part of the background, I’m sure — it’s a big change, and I don’t want to pretend it doesn’t affect me. But the thoughts don’t feel as simple as, “She left, so now I don’t want to wake up.”\n\nIt’s more like… I’ve been having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not necessarily in some dramatic way, but like going to sleep and not having to face everything again. And that scares me to say out loud, because it doesn’t feel like the way I usually think.\n\nThe thing that really frightened me was when I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was trying to make them understandable for someone else. Like I was getting things ready. I don’t even know if I fully realized what I was doing at first, but then it hit me, and I thought, why am I organizing this like somebody else is going to need to take over?\n\nSo… I can’t say it’s specifically just about my daughter leaving. It feels bigger and darker than that, and that’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more unsettling parts, because I used to be very good at that kind of thing. I mean, I spent years managing engineering spreadsheets — complicated ones, with formulas and dependencies and all these little details — and I didn’t really have to think about thinking, if that makes sense. I could just sit down and work through it.\n\nNow I’ll be looking at something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just… freeze. I can stare at it for half an hour and still not decide what the next step is. It’s not that I don’t understand what a bill is, obviously, or that I don’t know we need groceries. It’s more like my brain won’t organize the information into an action. I’ll read the same line over and over, or compare two things that shouldn’t be hard to compare, and somehow I still can’t make a decision.\n\nIt makes me feel embarrassed, honestly. Like I’ve lost access to a part of myself that used to be reliable.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had hard periods before, like anyone — stretches where I was stressed or down or not myself — but whether it was depressed nearly every day for a full two weeks before this past month… I don’t know.\n\nNothing is coming to mind that I can point to and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing, for that length of time. I’m trying not to rewrite my past just because things feel so bad now. There were definitely times I was unhappy or under strain, but I don’t know that I would describe it that way. This feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say I’ve been here before.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, do you remember ever having a previous episode like this?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve had bad times before, like I said — stressful periods, times where I was unhappy or overwhelmed — but I don’t remember ever having something that felt like this.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say “never” if there was some period I’m forgetting or minimizing, but nothing stands out to me as a previous episode like this. This past month feels different in a way that’s hard to compare to anything earlier.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not good. Really not good.\n\nI can get into bed, but then I’ll just lie there for hours. The house gets so quiet at night that it almost makes everything louder in my head, if that makes sense. I’m lying there listening to every little creak or the hum of something in the walls, and I’m trying to make myself sleep, but that just seems to make it worse.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up around three or four in the morning. It’s almost always that same window. I’ll come awake and for a few seconds I’ll think maybe I can just turn over and go back to sleep, but then my mind starts going. It runs through everything I’ve messed up, everything I should have done differently, things I said or didn’t say, decisions I made. Once that starts, that’s it. I just lie there staring into the dark, going over it again and again.\n\nSo I’m in bed for a long time, but it doesn’t feel like real rest. It feels like I spend half the night waiting to fall asleep and the other half trying not to think.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think so. I mean, her leaving for college is part of it, obviously — I don’t want to pretend it isn’t. It hit me harder than I expected, and I miss having her around in the house.\n\nBut this feels… bigger than that. Or darker than that. It’s not just “my daughter moved out and I’m sad.” It turns into this whole judgment on my life, like I failed as a father somehow, even though I know sending your child to college is supposed to be a good thing. I keep going over things I did wrong, things I didn’t do enough of, ways I should have been better. And then it spreads from that into this feeling that I’m not really useful to anyone anymore.\n\nThat’s honestly part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this thought that I’ve failed — as a father, as a person, just as someone who’s supposed to have some purpose. It loops in my head, and after a while it stops feeling like a thought and starts feeling like the truth. Like I don’t really deserve much of a future, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes… I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just her leaving. It feels like that may have opened the door, but what came through feels much heavier than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know you’re asking it a little more specifically now — like not just “a rough patch,” but two weeks or more where it was nearly every day, either feeling depressed or not really being interested in anything.\n\nWhen I think back, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing well, where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if it was nearly every day for two weeks, or if I’m sort of filling in the blanks afterward. It’s hard for me to separate what was actual depression from just being unhappy or stressed or withdrawn for a while.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a period before this where I’d say, yes, that definitely happened for at least two weeks nearly every day. But I also don’t feel completely confident saying no, because I may not have recognized it that way at the time. So… I guess my best answer is that I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind clearly enough for me to say yes.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function day to day?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. It’s not just that I’m moving slowly or having a bad few days — things are actually piling up in a way that’s starting to scare me.\n\nI’ve got bills I haven’t dealt with, not because I don’t know they’re there, but because I open the envelope or see the reminder and then I just… can’t make myself follow through. Same with things around the house. Little projects or repairs that I used to be able to chip away at are just sitting there, and every time I look at them I feel worse, but that still doesn’t make me do them.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve basically stopped showing up. I used to have book club, and sometimes rides with a couple people — not a huge circle, but there were a few people I counted on seeing. I’ve let that fall away. I either don’t respond or I tell myself I’ll go and then I don’t.\n\nI guess the part that makes me feel like I can’t just brush this off is… I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. That felt different. Like it crossed some line from “I’m behind on things” into “something is really not okay.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts of not wanting to wake up feel tied specifically to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s only that. I mean, my daughter leaving for college is part of the background, I’m sure — it’s a big change, and I don’t want to pretend it doesn’t affect me. But the thoughts don’t feel as simple as, “She left, so now I don’t want to wake up.”\n\nIt’s more like… I’ve been having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not necessarily in some dramatic way, but like going to sleep and not having to face everything again. And that scares me to say out loud, because it doesn’t feel like the way I usually think.\n\nThe thing that really frightened me was when I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was trying to make them understandable for someone else. Like I was getting things ready. I don’t even know if I fully realized what I was doing at first, but then it hit me, and I thought, why am I organizing this like somebody else is going to need to take over?\n\nSo… I can’t say it’s specifically just about my daughter leaving. It feels bigger and darker than that, and that’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more unsettling parts, because I used to be very good at that kind of thing. I mean, I spent years managing engineering spreadsheets — complicated ones, with formulas and dependencies and all these little details — and I didn’t really have to think about thinking, if that makes sense. I could just sit down and work through it.\n\nNow I’ll be looking at something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just… freeze. I can stare at it for half an hour and still not decide what the next step is. It’s not that I don’t understand what a bill is, obviously, or that I don’t know we need groceries. It’s more like my brain won’t organize the information into an action. I’ll read the same line over and over, or compare two things that shouldn’t be hard to compare, and somehow I still can’t make a decision.\n\nIt makes me feel embarrassed, honestly. Like I’ve lost access to a part of myself that used to be reliable.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had hard periods before, like anyone — stretches where I was stressed or down or not myself — but whether it was depressed nearly every day for a full two weeks before this past month… I don’t know.\n\nNothing is coming to mind that I can point to and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing, for that length of time. I’m trying not to rewrite my past just because things feel so bad now. There were definitely times I was unhappy or under strain, but I don’t know that I would describe it that way. This feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say I’ve been here before.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, do you remember ever having a previous episode like this?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can clearly remember. It loops in my head, and after a while it stops feeling like a thought and starts feeling like the truth. I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t been hearing voices, not like an actual voice in the room or someone talking to me when no one’s there.

But after she left, the house felt different in a way I didn’t expect. It was like this whole part of my life just… closed. And once she was gone, all I could think about was whether I’d done enough as her father, whether I’d wasted the time I had, whether she was leaving partly because she needed distance from me.\n\nSo, yeah, if I’m honest, that’s when this became constant. Around a month ago. Since she left.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much so. I used to have things I genuinely looked forward to, little things, but they mattered. I’ve had this model railway I’ve been working on for years—tracks, little buildings, wiring things up, painting tiny details. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but it used to calm me down. I could lose an afternoon in it and feel like I’d actually made something.\n\nNow it’s just sitting there on the table half-built. Pieces out, tools still there, like I meant to come back to it, but I don’t. I look at it and it just feels… pointless. Like I can’t understand why I cared about it before.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. I’ll sort of tell myself I’ll do it in a bit, and then the day just goes by, and by the time I realize I haven’t eaten, I don’t have the energy to make anything real.\n\nSo I end up with toast, or a bowl of cereal, something like that. Just whatever’s easiest and doesn’t feel like… an undertaking. And I’ve had to tighten my belt lately, which made me stop and think, because I hadn’t really been paying attention to it as “losing weight” exactly. It’s more like I’m just not eating normally.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had times in my life when I was unhappy, or under a lot of stress, or just… not myself for a while. I think most people probably have stretches like that, and when I look back it’s hard to know what counts as “depressed like this” versus just going through something difficult.\n\nWeeks at a time… maybe? But I don’t remember anything that feels clearly the same as what this has been like recently. I don’t want to say no too quickly, because my memory for those periods isn’t perfect, and I may have minimized things at the time. But I also can’t point to a definite past episode and say, “Yes, that was just like this.”\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Nothing stands out to me clearly before this past month in the same way.\n\nDoctor: At any earlier time in your life, did you ever have a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking it a little more specifically now — like not just “a rough patch,” but two weeks or more where it was nearly every day, either feeling depressed or not really being interested in anything.\n\nWhen I think back, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing well, where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if it was nearly every day for two weeks, or if I’m sort of filling in the blanks afterward. It’s hard for me to separate what was actual depression from just being unhappy or stressed or withdrawn for a while.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a period before this where I’d say, yes, that definitely happened for at least two weeks nearly every day. But I also don’t feel completely confident saying no, because I may not have recognized it that way at the time. So… I guess my best answer is that I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind clearly enough for me to say yes.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function day to day?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. It’s not just that I’m moving slowly or having a bad few days — things are actually piling up in a way that’s starting to scare me.\n\nI’ve got bills I haven’t dealt with, not because I don’t know they’re there, but because I open the envelope or see the reminder and then I just… can’t make myself follow through. Same with things around the house. Little projects or repairs that I used to be able to chip away at are just sitting there, and every time I look at them I feel worse, but that still doesn’t make me do them.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve basically stopped showing up. I used to have book club, and sometimes rides with a couple people — not a huge circle, but there were a few people I counted on seeing. I’ve let that fall away. I either don’t respond or I tell myself I’ll go and then I don’t.\n\nI guess the part that makes me feel like I can’t just brush this off is… I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. That felt different. Like it crossed some line from “I’m behind on things” into “something is really not okay.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts of not wanting to wake up feel tied specifically to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s only that. I mean, my daughter leaving for college is part of the background, I’m sure — it’s a big change, and I don’t want to pretend it doesn’t affect me. But the thoughts don’t feel as simple as, “She left, so now I don’t want to wake up.”\n\nIt’s more like… I’ve been having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not necessarily in some dramatic way, but like going to sleep and not having to face everything again. And that scares me to say out loud, because it doesn’t feel like the way I usually think.\n\nThe thing that really frightened me was when I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was trying to make them understandable for someone else. Like I was getting things ready. I don’t even know if I fully realized what I was doing at first, but then it hit me, and I thought, why am I organizing this like somebody else is going to need to take over?\n\nSo… I can’t say it’s specifically just about my daughter leaving. It feels bigger and darker than that, and that’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more unsettling parts, because I used to be very good at that kind of thing. I mean, I spent years managing engineering spreadsheets — complicated ones, with formulas and dependencies and all these little details — and I didn’t really have to think about thinking, if that makes sense. I could just sit down and work through it.\n\nNow I’ll be looking at something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just… freeze. I can stare at it for half an hour and still not decide what the next step is. It’s not that I don’t understand what a bill is, obviously, or that I don’t know we need groceries. It’s more like my brain won’t organize the information into an action. I’ll read the same line over and over, or compare two things that shouldn’t be hard to compare, and somehow I still can’t make a decision.\n\nIt makes me feel embarrassed, honestly. Like I’ve lost access to a part of myself that used to be reliable.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had hard periods before, like anyone — stretches where I was stressed or down or not myself — but whether it was depressed nearly every day for a full two weeks before this past month… I don’t know.\n\nNothing is coming to mind that I can point to and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing, for that length of time. I’m trying not to rewrite my past just because things feel so bad now. There were definitely times I was unhappy or under strain, but I don’t know that I would describe it that way. This feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say I’ve been here before.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, do you remember ever having a previous episode like this?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve had bad times before, like I said — stressful periods, times where I was unhappy or overwhelmed — but I don’t remember ever having something that felt like this.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say “never” if there was some period I’m forgetting or minimizing, but nothing stands out to me as a previous episode like this. This past month feels different in a way that’s hard to compare to anything earlier.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not good. Really not good.\n\nI can get into bed, but then I’ll just lie there for hours. The house gets so quiet at night that it almost makes everything louder in my head, if that makes sense. I’m lying there listening to every little creak or the hum of something in the walls, and I’m trying to make myself sleep, but that just seems to make it worse.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up around three or four in the morning. It’s almost always that same window. I’ll come awake and for a few seconds I’ll think maybe I can just turn over and go back to sleep, but then my mind starts going. It runs through everything I’ve messed up, everything I should have done differently, things I said or didn’t say, decisions I made. Once that starts, that’s it. I just lie there staring into the dark, going over it again and again.\n\nSo I’m in bed for a long time, but it doesn’t feel like real rest. It feels like I spend half the night waiting to fall asleep and the other half trying not to think.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think so. I mean, her leaving for college is part of it, obviously — I don’t want to pretend it isn’t. It hit me harder than I expected, and I miss having her around in the house.\n\nBut this feels… bigger than that. Or darker than that. It’s not just “my daughter moved out and I’m sad.” It turns into this whole judgment on my life, like I failed as a father somehow, even though I know sending your child to college is supposed to be a good thing. I keep going over things I did wrong, things I didn’t do enough of, ways I should have been better. And then it spreads from that into this feeling that I’m not really useful to anyone anymore.\n\nThat’s honestly part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this thought that I’ve failed — as a father, as a person, just as someone who’s supposed to have some purpose. It loops in my head, and after a while it stops feeling like a thought and starts feeling like the truth. Like I don’t really deserve much of a future, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes… I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just her leaving. It feels like that may have opened the door, but what came through feels much heavier than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices or becoming convinced of things that others would say aren’t true, such as that you are being punished or watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t been hearing voices, not like an actual voice in the room or someone talking to me when no one’s there.\n\nAnd I don’t think I’m being watched or anything like that. The “punished” part is harder to answer, because emotionally I do sometimes feel like I must deserve how awful I feel, if that makes sense. But I know that’s more of a feeling, or a thought I get stuck on, not that I literally believe someone or something is punishing me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve been convinced of things that other people would say aren’t true. It’s more that my own thoughts get very harsh and convincing, but I still know they’re thoughts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes. Very much.

The “punished” part is harder to answer, because emotionally I do sometimes feel like I must deserve how awful I feel, if that makes sense. But I know that’s more of a feeling, or a thought I get stuck on, not that I literally believe someone or something is punishing me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve been convinced of things that other people would say aren’t true. It’s more that my own thoughts get very harsh and convincing, but I still know they’re thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much.\n\nIt’s not just feeling sad about things. It’s more like… I look ahead and I can’t really picture anything getting better, or I don’t feel like I’ve earned the right to expect it to. I keep circling back to this idea that I failed — as a father, and just as a useful person in general. That’s honestly a big reason I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from it.\n\nIt loops in my head over and over, and after a while it starts to feel less like I’m having a bad thought and more like I’m just seeing the truth clearly. It feels like that may have opened the door, but what came through feels much heavier than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices or becoming convinced of things that others would say aren’t true, such as that you are being punished or watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t been hearing voices, not like an actual voice in the room or someone talking to me when no one’s there.\n\nAnd I don’t think I’m being watched or anything like that. The “punished” part is harder to answer, because emotionally I do sometimes feel like I must deserve how awful I feel, if that makes sense. But I know that’s more of a feeling, or a thought I get stuck on, not that I literally believe someone or something is punishing me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve been convinced of things that other people would say aren’t true. It’s more that my own thoughts get very harsh and convincing, but I still know they’re thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much.\n\nIt’s not just feeling sad about things. It’s more like… I look ahead and I can’t really picture anything getting better, or I don’t feel like I’ve earned the right to expect it to. I keep circling back to this idea that I failed — as a father, and just as a useful person in general. That’s honestly a big reason I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from it.\n\nIt loops in my head over and over, and after a while it starts to feel less like I’m having a bad thought and more like I’m just seeing the truth clearly. Like I’ve already messed up the important parts, and there isn’t much future I deserve. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you thinking of killing yourself right now?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"I've been having thoughts that it would be easier if I didn't wake up, and it scared me when I caught myself going over my finances like I was getting things ready for someone else.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Right now… I don’t know if I would say I’m about to do something this minute. But I have been thinking about death, yes. More than I want to admit.

A lot of it is this thought that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like, not having to keep doing this, not having to face the same thoughts again in the morning. And I know that sounds bad, and it scares me to say it out loud.

What really frightened me was that I caught myself going over my finances — not in the normal way, not just bills or planning — but almost like I was trying to make sure things would be in order for someone else. Like I was preparing things. And then I realized what I was doing and it really shook me, because it felt too close to something more serious than just wishing I could disappear for a while.

So… yes, I’m having thoughts like that. I’m scared of them. I don’t want to feel this way, but they’re there.

It’s not something I’ve clearly noticed the way I’ve noticed some of the other things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has decreased?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think it has. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to, or maybe I just don’t notice it until it’s already late. Most days I don’t bother making a proper meal. I’ll sort of tell myself I’ll do it in a bit, and then the day just goes by, and by the time I realize I haven’t eaten, I don’t have the energy to make anything real.\n\nSo I end up with toast, or a bowl of cereal, something like that. Just whatever’s easiest and doesn’t feel like… an undertaking. And I’ve had to tighten my belt lately, which made me stop and think, because I hadn’t really been paying attention to it as “losing weight” exactly. It’s more like I’m just not eating normally.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had times in my life when I was unhappy, or under a lot of stress, or just… not myself for a while. I think most people probably have stretches like that, and when I look back it’s hard to know what counts as “depressed like this” versus just going through something difficult.\n\nWeeks at a time… maybe? But I don’t remember anything that feels clearly the same as what this has been like recently. I don’t want to say no too quickly, because my memory for those periods isn’t perfect, and I may have minimized things at the time. But I also can’t point to a definite past episode and say, “Yes, that was just like this.”\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Nothing stands out to me clearly before this past month in the same way.\n\nDoctor: At any earlier time in your life, did you ever have a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking it a little more specifically now — like not just “a rough patch,” but two weeks or more where it was nearly every day, either feeling depressed or not really being interested in anything.\n\nWhen I think back, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing well, where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if it was nearly every day for two weeks, or if I’m sort of filling in the blanks afterward. It’s hard for me to separate what was actual depression from just being unhappy or stressed or withdrawn for a while.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a period before this where I’d say, yes, that definitely happened for at least two weeks nearly every day. But I also don’t feel completely confident saying no, because I may not have recognized it that way at the time. So… I guess my best answer is that I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind clearly enough for me to say yes.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function day to day?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. It’s not just that I’m moving slowly or having a bad few days — things are actually piling up in a way that’s starting to scare me.\n\nI’ve got bills I haven’t dealt with, not because I don’t know they’re there, but because I open the envelope or see the reminder and then I just… can’t make myself follow through. Same with things around the house. Little projects or repairs that I used to be able to chip away at are just sitting there, and every time I look at them I feel worse, but that still doesn’t make me do them.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve basically stopped showing up. I used to have book club, and sometimes rides with a couple people — not a huge circle, but there were a few people I counted on seeing. I’ve let that fall away. I either don’t respond or I tell myself I’ll go and then I don’t.\n\nI guess the part that makes me feel like I can’t just brush this off is… I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. That felt different. Like it crossed some line from “I’m behind on things” into “something is really not okay.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts of not wanting to wake up feel tied specifically to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s only that. I mean, my daughter leaving for college is part of the background, I’m sure — it’s a big change, and I don’t want to pretend it doesn’t affect me. But the thoughts don’t feel as simple as, “She left, so now I don’t want to wake up.”\n\nIt’s more like… I’ve been having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not necessarily in some dramatic way, but like going to sleep and not having to face everything again. And that scares me to say out loud, because it doesn’t feel like the way I usually think.\n\nThe thing that really frightened me was when I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was trying to make them understandable for someone else. Like I was getting things ready. I don’t even know if I fully realized what I was doing at first, but then it hit me, and I thought, why am I organizing this like somebody else is going to need to take over?\n\nSo… I can’t say it’s specifically just about my daughter leaving. It feels bigger and darker than that, and that’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more unsettling parts, because I used to be very good at that kind of thing. I mean, I spent years managing engineering spreadsheets — complicated ones, with formulas and dependencies and all these little details — and I didn’t really have to think about thinking, if that makes sense. I could just sit down and work through it.\n\nNow I’ll be looking at something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just… freeze. I can stare at it for half an hour and still not decide what the next step is. It’s not that I don’t understand what a bill is, obviously, or that I don’t know we need groceries. It’s more like my brain won’t organize the information into an action. I’ll read the same line over and over, or compare two things that shouldn’t be hard to compare, and somehow I still can’t make a decision.\n\nIt makes me feel embarrassed, honestly. Like I’ve lost access to a part of myself that used to be reliable.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had hard periods before, like anyone — stretches where I was stressed or down or not myself — but whether it was depressed nearly every day for a full two weeks before this past month… I don’t know.\n\nNothing is coming to mind that I can point to and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing, for that length of time. I’m trying not to rewrite my past just because things feel so bad now. There were definitely times I was unhappy or under strain, but I don’t know that I would describe it that way. This feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say I’ve been here before.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, do you remember ever having a previous episode like this?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve had bad times before, like I said — stressful periods, times where I was unhappy or overwhelmed — but I don’t remember ever having something that felt like this.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say “never” if there was some period I’m forgetting or minimizing, but nothing stands out to me as a previous episode like this. This past month feels different in a way that’s hard to compare to anything earlier.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not good. Really not good.\n\nI can get into bed, but then I’ll just lie there for hours. The house gets so quiet at night that it almost makes everything louder in my head, if that makes sense. I’m lying there listening to every little creak or the hum of something in the walls, and I’m trying to make myself sleep, but that just seems to make it worse.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up around three or four in the morning. It’s almost always that same window. I’ll come awake and for a few seconds I’ll think maybe I can just turn over and go back to sleep, but then my mind starts going. It runs through everything I’ve messed up, everything I should have done differently, things I said or didn’t say, decisions I made. Once that starts, that’s it. I just lie there staring into the dark, going over it again and again.\n\nSo I’m in bed for a long time, but it doesn’t feel like real rest. It feels like I spend half the night waiting to fall asleep and the other half trying not to think.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think so. I mean, her leaving for college is part of it, obviously — I don’t want to pretend it isn’t. It hit me harder than I expected, and I miss having her around in the house.\n\nBut this feels… bigger than that. Or darker than that. It’s not just “my daughter moved out and I’m sad.” It turns into this whole judgment on my life, like I failed as a father somehow, even though I know sending your child to college is supposed to be a good thing. I keep going over things I did wrong, things I didn’t do enough of, ways I should have been better. And then it spreads from that into this feeling that I’m not really useful to anyone anymore.\n\nThat’s honestly part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this thought that I’ve failed — as a father, as a person, just as someone who’s supposed to have some purpose. It loops in my head, and after a while it stops feeling like a thought and starts feeling like the truth. Like I don’t really deserve much of a future, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes… I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just her leaving. It feels like that may have opened the door, but what came through feels much heavier than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices or becoming convinced of things that others would say aren’t true, such as that you are being punished or watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. The “punished” part is harder to answer, because emotionally I do sometimes feel like I must deserve how awful I feel, if that makes sense. But I know that’s more of a feeling, or a thought I get stuck on, not that I literally believe someone or something is punishing me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve been convinced of things that other people would say aren’t true. It’s more that my own thoughts get very harsh and convincing, but I still know they’re thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much.\n\nIt’s not just feeling sad about things. It’s more like… I look ahead and I can’t really picture anything getting better, or I don’t feel like I’ve earned the right to expect it to. I keep circling back to this idea that I failed — as a father, and just as a useful person in general. That’s honestly a big reason I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from it.\n\nIt loops in my head over and over, and after a while it starts to feel less like I’m having a bad thought and more like I’m just seeing the truth clearly. Like I’ve already messed up the important parts, and there isn’t much future I deserve. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking of killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now… I don’t know if I would say I’m about to do something this minute. Like, not having to keep doing this, not having to face the same thoughts again in the morning. Like I was preparing things. And then I realized what I was doing and it really shook me, because it felt too close to something more serious than just wishing I could disappear for a while.\n\nSo… yes, I’m having thoughts like that. Yes… at least two weeks. It’s been closer to a month now, I think.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is this feeling I can’t get away from, that I’ve failed. I used to have things I genuinely looked forward to, little things, but they mattered. I’ve had this model railway I’ve been working on for years—tracks, little buildings, wiring things up, painting tiny details. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, but it used to calm me down. I could lose an afternoon in it and feel like I’d actually made something.\n\nNow it’s just sitting there on the table half-built. Pieces out, tools still there, like I meant to come back to it, but I don’t. I look at it and it just feels… pointless. Like I can’t understand why I cared about it before.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least text during the games if we weren’t in the same room. That was kind of our thing. Now even opening a game on TV feels like too much, or like there’s no reason to. I’ll think, “Maybe I should put it on,” and then I just don’t, because it feels empty before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the day nearly every day” sounds so specific, and I haven’t exactly been keeping track by the hour.\n\nI know I’ve been feeling low a lot. It’s not like one bad afternoon where I can say, “Oh, that was a rough day,” and then I bounce back. It’s more like this weight is there when I wake up, and then sometimes it fades into the background if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really leave. I’ll be sitting there doing nothing in particular and realize I’ve just been feeling awful for… I don’t even know how long.\n\nSo, yes, I think so. Or close to that. It feels like it’s been there most days, and for a lot of each day. I don’t want to exaggerate, but I also don’t want to pretend it’s just occasional, because it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like… I haven’t had someone say, “You’re talking slower,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I do feel like I take longer to answer sometimes because I’m trying to get my thoughts together, and I probably pause more than I used to. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as moving or speaking noticeably slowly. I haven’t really been watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I can’t say for sure. It’s not something I’ve clearly noticed the way I’ve noticed some of the other things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has decreased?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think it has. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to, or maybe I just don’t notice it until it’s already late. Most days I don’t bother making a proper meal. I’ll sort of tell myself I’ll do it in a bit, and then the day just goes by, and by the time I realize I haven’t eaten, I don’t have the energy to make anything real.\n\nSo I end up with toast, or a bowl of cereal, something like that. Just whatever’s easiest and doesn’t feel like… an undertaking. And I’ve had to tighten my belt lately, which made me stop and think, because I hadn’t really been paying attention to it as “losing weight” exactly. It’s more like I’m just not eating normally.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had times in my life when I was unhappy, or under a lot of stress, or just… not myself for a while. I think most people probably have stretches like that, and when I look back it’s hard to know what counts as “depressed like this” versus just going through something difficult.\n\nWeeks at a time… maybe? But I don’t remember anything that feels clearly the same as what this has been like recently. I don’t want to say no too quickly, because my memory for those periods isn’t perfect, and I may have minimized things at the time. But I also can’t point to a definite past episode and say, “Yes, that was just like this.”\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Nothing stands out to me clearly before this past month in the same way.\n\nDoctor: At any earlier time in your life, did you ever have a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that cleanly. I know you’re asking it a little more specifically now — like not just “a rough patch,” but two weeks or more where it was nearly every day, either feeling depressed or not really being interested in anything.\n\nWhen I think back, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing well, where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if it was nearly every day for two weeks, or if I’m sort of filling in the blanks afterward. It’s hard for me to separate what was actual depression from just being unhappy or stressed or withdrawn for a while.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory of a period before this where I’d say, yes, that definitely happened for at least two weeks nearly every day. But I also don’t feel completely confident saying no, because I may not have recognized it that way at the time. So… I guess my best answer is that I don’t know. Nothing comes to mind clearly enough for me to say yes.\n\nDoctor: How much has this been interfering with your ability to function day to day?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. It’s not just that I’m moving slowly or having a bad few days — things are actually piling up in a way that’s starting to scare me.\n\nI’ve got bills I haven’t dealt with, not because I don’t know they’re there, but because I open the envelope or see the reminder and then I just… can’t make myself follow through. Same with things around the house. Little projects or repairs that I used to be able to chip away at are just sitting there, and every time I look at them I feel worse, but that still doesn’t make me do them.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve basically stopped showing up. I used to have book club, and sometimes rides with a couple people — not a huge circle, but there were a few people I counted on seeing. I’ve let that fall away. I either don’t respond or I tell myself I’ll go and then I don’t.\n\nI guess the part that makes me feel like I can’t just brush this off is… I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. That felt different. Like it crossed some line from “I’m behind on things” into “something is really not okay.”\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts of not wanting to wake up feel tied specifically to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s only that. I mean, my daughter leaving for college is part of the background, I’m sure — it’s a big change, and I don’t want to pretend it doesn’t affect me. But the thoughts don’t feel as simple as, “She left, so now I don’t want to wake up.”\n\nIt’s more like… I’ve been having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not necessarily in some dramatic way, but like going to sleep and not having to face everything again. And that scares me to say out loud, because it doesn’t feel like the way I usually think.\n\nThe thing that really frightened me was when I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was trying to make them understandable for someone else. Like I was getting things ready. I don’t even know if I fully realized what I was doing at first, but then it hit me, and I thought, why am I organizing this like somebody else is going to need to take over?\n\nSo… I can’t say it’s specifically just about my daughter leaving. It feels bigger and darker than that, and that’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more unsettling parts, because I used to be very good at that kind of thing. I mean, I spent years managing engineering spreadsheets — complicated ones, with formulas and dependencies and all these little details — and I didn’t really have to think about thinking, if that makes sense. I could just sit down and work through it.\n\nNow I’ll be looking at something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just… freeze. I can stare at it for half an hour and still not decide what the next step is. It’s not that I don’t understand what a bill is, obviously, or that I don’t know we need groceries. It’s more like my brain won’t organize the information into an action. I’ll read the same line over and over, or compare two things that shouldn’t be hard to compare, and somehow I still can’t make a decision.\n\nIt makes me feel embarrassed, honestly. Like I’ve lost access to a part of myself that used to be reliable.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you were depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve had hard periods before, like anyone — stretches where I was stressed or down or not myself — but whether it was depressed nearly every day for a full two weeks before this past month… I don’t know.\n\nNothing is coming to mind that I can point to and say, yes, that was the same kind of thing, for that length of time. I’m trying not to rewrite my past just because things feel so bad now. There were definitely times I was unhappy or under strain, but I don’t know that I would describe it that way. This feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say I’ve been here before.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, do you remember ever having a previous episode like this?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve had bad times before, like I said — stressful periods, times where I was unhappy or overwhelmed — but I don’t remember ever having something that felt like this.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say “never” if there was some period I’m forgetting or minimizing, but nothing stands out to me as a previous episode like this. This past month feels different in a way that’s hard to compare to anything earlier.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not good. Really not good.\n\nI can get into bed, but then I’ll just lie there for hours. The house gets so quiet at night that it almost makes everything louder in my head, if that makes sense. I’m lying there listening to every little creak or the hum of something in the walls, and I’m trying to make myself sleep, but that just seems to make it worse.\n\nAnd then if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up around three or four in the morning. It’s almost always that same window. I’ll come awake and for a few seconds I’ll think maybe I can just turn over and go back to sleep, but then my mind starts going. It runs through everything I’ve messed up, everything I should have done differently, things I said or didn’t say, decisions I made. Once that starts, that’s it. I just lie there staring into the dark, going over it again and again.\n\nSo I’m in bed for a long time, but it doesn’t feel like real rest. It feels like I spend half the night waiting to fall asleep and the other half trying not to think.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect from your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think so. I mean, her leaving for college is part of it, obviously — I don’t want to pretend it isn’t. It hit me harder than I expected, and I miss having her around in the house.\n\nBut this feels… bigger than that. Or darker than that. It’s not just “my daughter moved out and I’m sad.” It turns into this whole judgment on my life, like I failed as a father somehow, even though I know sending your child to college is supposed to be a good thing. I keep going over things I did wrong, things I didn’t do enough of, ways I should have been better. And then it spreads from that into this feeling that I’m not really useful to anyone anymore.\n\nThat’s honestly part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this thought that I’ve failed — as a father, as a person, just as someone who’s supposed to have some purpose. It loops in my head, and after a while it stops feeling like a thought and starts feeling like the truth. Like I don’t really deserve much of a future, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes… I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just her leaving. It feels like that may have opened the door, but what came through feels much heavier than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices or becoming convinced of things that others would say aren’t true, such as that you are being punished or watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t been hearing voices, not like an actual voice in the room or someone talking to me when no one’s there.\n\nAnd I don’t think I’m being watched or anything like that. The “punished” part is harder to answer, because emotionally I do sometimes feel like I must deserve how awful I feel, if that makes sense. But I know that’s more of a feeling, or a thought I get stuck on, not that I literally believe someone or something is punishing me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve been convinced of things that other people would say aren’t true. It’s more that my own thoughts get very harsh and convincing, but I still know they’re thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much.\n\nIt’s not just feeling sad about things. It’s more like… I look ahead and I can’t really picture anything getting better, or I don’t feel like I’ve earned the right to expect it to. I keep circling back to this idea that I failed — as a father, and just as a useful person in general. That’s honestly a big reason I came in, because I can’t seem to get away from it.\n\nIt loops in my head over and over, and after a while it starts to feel less like I’m having a bad thought and more like I’m just seeing the truth clearly. Like I’ve already messed up the important parts, and there isn’t much future I deserve. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking of killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now… I don’t know if I would say I’m about to do something this minute. But I have been thinking about death, yes. More than I want to admit.\n\nA lot of it is this thought that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like, not having to keep doing this, not having to face the same thoughts again in the morning. Like I was preparing things.
